Um there's a bunch of things that are going to affect carryover cooking. Um so yes, mass is one of them. So the the more mass of your meat, um so well, first of all, carryover cooking, if you if you're unfamiliar with it, it's the idea that once you take your meat off heat, say out of the oven or out of the pan, um the core temperature might be a specific temperature then, but the rest of the meat is hotter than that. You know, generally meat cooks from the outside in. Uh and so when your core is at 120, there's still layers on the outside that are going to be hotter. um some of that heat will dissipate into the room. Um but some of it's also going to work its way into the meat. Uh and so that core temperature will continue to rise even after you get it out of its heat source. Um there are a lot of variables involved in this and which makes it which is what makes it so hard to predict. Um generally, yeah, the more mass of your meat, uh the more thermal mass it has and so the more heat there is going to be to work its way in. So a big prime uh prime rib roasted in the oven is going to carry over more than a steak roasted in the oven. um the the hotter you roast it um the bigger the sort of gradient you're building inside and so um the more again the more carryover you're going to have. Um, but if you're race roasting say at in a 500°ree oven, by the time your center gets to 120°, those outer layers might be at 180, 190, um, maybe even higher than that. ## 13. Non-stick Pan Comparison: Teflon vs. Cast Iron vs. Our Place Titanium Pro
**Channel:** J. Kenji Lopez-Alt | **Views:** 67K | **Date:** 2 months ago | **Duration:** 30:59 | **ID:** aXmKAsE3_Ec
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=aXmKAsE3_Ec

### Transcript:
Hey everyone, it's Kenji. Uh, and today I want to talk about non-stick pans. So, I have in front of me three different non-stick pans. The first one is a teflon coated pan or a teflon style pan. Uh, this has a chemical coating on the inside uh that's essentially sort of sprayed on. This is what I think most people think of when they think about non-stick. Uh, the second one here is a cast iron pan. So, this is a vintage Wagner pan. It's got a nice smooth finish. Um, it's been well seasoned, which means that the oil has polymerized and given it a nice slick non-stick coating on the inside. So, this one, it's a little bit harder to come by these, but they should be pretty non-stick, too. Uh, this final one is the new titanium pro pan. Uh, this one was a collaboration between myself uh and the company our place in LA. Um, this one uses a new technology. So, it's a a clad pan, which means it's an aluminum core claded with steel on the outside, um, with a layer of titanium on the cooking surface, which offers it some non-stick properties as well. Um, so, because I've partnered with this company to produce this pan, um, it's essentially, you know, my my pan. So, I understand that it's going to be difficult for people to believe me when I just say that this pan uh, offers a lot of advantages over the other ones. So, today I want to run through a series of tests. I'm going to do them all side by side to show you in exactly which department each different type of pan sort of excels uh where one type of pan might do better than the other. Um so that way you get a sort of fair look at all the pans and can decide for yourself based on your own sort of cooking styles what type of non-stick pan might suit you best. Um so let's get into the testing. All right. So the first one is the evenness test. So what I've done is I've put a tortilla in the bottom of each one of these pans. I'm going to put a layer of cheese on top just to sort of weigh them down and make sure uh that they maintain contact uh with the bottom of the pan. Um and also so that we can fold them into quesadillas when we're done and eat them. But what I'm going to do is I'm going to put this tortilla down here. Going to put the cheese on top. ## 14. How to Cook the Juiciest Beef Tenderloin
**Channel:** J. Kenji Lopez-Alt | **Views:** 87K | **Date:** 2 months ago | **Duration:** 17:06 | **ID:** wPbeYYur3tI
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=wPbeYYur3tI

### Transcript:
Hey everyone, it's Kenji. We're going to be cooking some tenderloin. So, you know, a tenderloin roast, I think it's great for the holidays. I got here a small roast that I cooked the traditional way. So, I seared this in a skillet and then I finished it off in a hot oven. That's the normal way that you would cook a tenderloin. As I was cooking it, I placed a combustion ink predictive thermometer inside which told me exactly when it got to 120° in the center, which is a nice rare for tenderloin. Looks beautiful on the outside. And when I pull this open, we're going to see that it is perfectly medium rare in the center, really nice and juicy pink. But as we get towards those sort of outer edges, it becomes increasingly more and more well done. In fact, using the data from the temperature probe, I know that by the time the center was at 120°, the outer layers were up to 185°. So, that is very, very overcooked. What I want to show you today is a better way to cook a tenderloin roast. Um, it's the reverse sear. Um, and so essentially what we're going to do is reverse the cooking process. So rather than starting it in a hot pan and finish it in the oven, we're going to start it in a relatively low temperature oven and finish it in a hot pan. What that's going to do for us is get us um a roast that is much more evenly cooked from edge to center. It's also going to preclude the need to rest the meat and it's going to give us a better sear on the surface. Um, so here is how we do that. So, to show you the difference between what happens when you cook a tenderloin the traditional way versus the reverse here, we're going to use these combustion ink predictive thermometers for my friend Chris Young. Um, it will show us exactly what's going on inside the meat. And so, this thermometer has sensors all up and down its length. Seven different sensors. So, we'll be able to see what the meat looks like at the very center um all the way to the end of the meat. Um, and we can compare the traditional method versus uh the reverse sear. Anyway, what we're going to do first is prep this meat for cooking. So, this is a whole beef tenderloin that we got. Um, just took it out of its packaging.
